<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stephen_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Stability_and_violence">Stability and violence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stephen_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Stability and violence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Stephen reigned for more than 47 years,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolovanConstantiniuMichelsonPop1997118_114-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolovanConstantiniuMichelsonPop1997118-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources. (July 2017)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESacerdoțeanu196939_200-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESacerdoțeanu196939-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> which was "in itself an outstanding achievement in the context of the political and territorial fragility of the Romanian principalities".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201433_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201433-229">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> His diplomacy evidenced that he was one of the "most astute politicians" of Europe in the 15th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolovanConstantiniuMichelsonPop1997118_114-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolovanConstantiniuMichelsonPop1997118-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources. (July 2017)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> This skill enabled him to play off the Ottoman Empire, Poland and Hungary against each other.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBolovanConstantiniuMichelsonPop1997118_114-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBolovanConstantiniuMichelsonPop1997118-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources. (July 2017)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> According to historian <a href="/wiki/Keith_Hitchins" title="Keith Hitchins">Keith Hitchins</a>, Stephen "paid tribute to the Ottomans, but only when it was advantageous...; he did homage to King Casimir of Poland as his suzerain when that seemed wise ...; and he resorted to arms when other means failed."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHitchins201429_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201429-230">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stephen suppressed the rebellious boyars and strengthened central government, often applying cruel punishments, including <a href="/wiki/Impalement" title="Impalement">impalement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201436–37_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201436–37-231">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> He consolidated the <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_Romania" title="Slavery in Romania">practice of slavery</a>, including the notion that different laws applied to slaves, reportedly capturing as many as 17,000 <a href="/wiki/Romani_people_in_Romania" title="Romani people in Romania">Roma</a> during his invasion of Wallachia, but also selectively freeing and assimilating Tatar slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAchim200417,_35–36_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAchim200417,_35–36-232">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> He supposedly used both communities as "slaves of the court", treasuring their specialized skills;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEmandi1994322_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmandi1994322-233">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> nevertheless, one folk legend additionally claims that Stephen practiced <a href="/wiki/Human_sacrifice" title="Human sacrifice">human sacrifice</a> against Roma slaves, to alleviate the floods at <a href="/wiki/Suli%C8%9Ba" title="Sulița">Sulița</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOișteanu2009428_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOișteanu2009428-234">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Marcin_Bielski" title="Marcin Bielski">Marcin Bielski</a>, during the 1498 expedition to Poland, the voivode participated in, or at least tolerated, the capture of as many as 100,000 people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECiubotaru200570–71_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECiubotaru200570–71-235">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGorovei2014407–408_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGorovei2014407–408-236">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> At least some of these were colonized in Moldavia, where, according to various reports of the period, they founded "<a href="/wiki/Ruthenians" title="Ruthenians">Ruthenian</a>" <a href="/wiki/T%C3%A2rg" title="Târg">undefended towns</a>. According to historian Mircea Ciubotaru, these may include Cernauca (now <a href="/wiki/Chornivka" title="Chornivka">Chornivka</a> in Ukraine), <a href="/wiki/Dobrov%C4%83%C8%9B" title="Dobrovăț">Dobrovăț</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lipnic" title="Lipnic">Lipnic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Letea_Veche" title="Letea Veche">Ruși-Ciutea</a>, and a cluster of villages outside <a href="/wiki/H%C3%A2rl%C4%83u" title="Hârlău">Hârlău</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECiubotaru200570–77_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECiubotaru200570–77-237">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stephen also welcomed freemen as settlers, establishing some of the first <a href="/wiki/Armenians_of_Romania" title="Armenians of Romania">Armenian</a> colonies in Moldavia, including one at Suceava,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESiruni194411,_19,_25,_61_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESiruni194411,_19,_25,_61-238">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup> while also settling <a href="/wiki/Italians_in_Romania" title="Italians in Romania">Italians</a>, some of whom were escapees from the <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_the_Ottoman_Empire" title="Slavery in the Ottoman Empire">Ottoman slave trade</a>, in that city.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECiubotaru200571–72_239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECiubotaru200571–72-239">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEmandi1994321_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmandi1994321-240">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> Early on, he renewed the commercial privileges of <a href="/wiki/Transylvanian_Saxons" title="Transylvanian Saxons">Transylvanian Saxons</a> who traded in Moldavia, but subsequently introduced some protectionist barriers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDemciuc20043_29-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDemciuc20043-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> His own court was staffed with foreign experts, among them Matteo Muriano and the Italian banker Dorino Cattaneo.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEmandi1994321_240-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmandi1994321-240">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESiruni194427_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESiruni194427-241">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup> However, as a "crusader" in the 1470s, Stephen encouraged the religious persecution and extortion of <a href="/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church" title="Armenian Apostolic Church">Gregorian Armenians</a>, <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Romania" title="History of the Jews in Romania">Jews</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Hussites" title="Hussites">Hussites</a>, some of whom became supporters of the Ottoman Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESimon2009233–239_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESimon2009233–239-242">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition to his colonization policies, Stephen restored Crown lands that had been lost during the civil war that followed Alexander the Good's rule, either through buying or confiscating them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199625_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199625-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201438–39_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201438–39-243">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> On the other hand, he granted much landed property to the Church and to the lesser noblemen who were the main supporters of the central government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199625–26_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199625–26-244">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> His itinerant lifestyle enabled him to personally hold court in the whole of Moldavia, which contributed to the development of his authority.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201439_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201439-245">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When talking with Muriano in 1502, Stephen mentioned that he had fought 36 battles, only losing two of them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201451_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201451-246">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> When the enemy forces mostly outnumbered his army, Stephen had to adopt the tactics of "<a href="/wiki/Asymmetric_warfare" title="Asymmetric warfare">asymmetric warfare</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201454_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201454-247">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> He practised <a href="/wiki/Guerrilla_warfare" title="Guerrilla warfare">guerilla warfare</a> against invaders, avoiding challenging them to open battle before they were weakened due to the lack of supplies or sickness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201452_248-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201452-248">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> During his invasions, however, he moved quickly and forced his enemies to do battle.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201452_248-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201452-248">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> To strengthen the defence of his country, he restored the fortresses built during Alexander the Good's rule at Hotin, Chilia, Cetatea Albă, Suceava and Târgu Neamț.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201441–42_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201441–42-249">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> He also erected a number of castles, including the new fortresses at Roman and <a href="/wiki/Tighina_Fortress" title="Tighina Fortress">Tighina</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201442_69-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201442-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The <i><a href="/wiki/Burgrave" title="Burgrave">pârcălabi</a></i> (or commanders) of the fortresses were invested with administrative and judicial powers and became important pillars of royal administration,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199627_250-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199627-250">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> their work controlled by a new central office, the <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arma%C8%99&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Armaș (page does not exist)">armaș</a></i> (first attested in 1489).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDemciuc20043_29-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDemciuc20043-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The <i>pârcălabi</i> included members of the princely family, such as Duma, who was Stephen's cousin;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEșanu2013136_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEșanu2013136-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> before his execution, Isaia, the voivode's brother-in-law, had supervised Chilia<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDemciuc20044_3-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDemciuc20044-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Neam%C8%9B_Citadel" title="Neamț Citadel">Neamț Citadel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESacerdoțeanu196940_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESacerdoțeanu196940-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stephen hired mercenaries to man his forts, which diminished the military role of the boyars' retinues within the <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_military_forces" title="Moldavian military forces">Moldavian military forces</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201441_251-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201441-251">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> He also set up a personal guard 3,000 strong<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201441_251-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201441-251">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> and, at least for a while, an Armenian-only unit.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESimon2009233_252-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESimon2009233-252">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> To strengthen the defence of Moldavia, he obliged the peasantry to bear arms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199628_253-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199628-253">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> Moldavian chronicles recorded that if "he found a peasant lacking arrows, bow or sword, or coming to the army without spurs for the horse, he mercilessly put that man to death."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199628_253-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199628-253">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> The military reforms increased Moldavia's military potential, enabling Stephen to muster an army of more than 40,000 strong.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199628–29_254-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199628–29-254">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cultural_development">Cultural development</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stephen_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Cultural development">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"/><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:408px;max-width:408px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Letopise%C8%9Bul_de_la_Bistri%C8%9Ba.svg" class="image"><img alt="Handwritten page with Slavonic lettering" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Letopise%C8%9Bul_de_la_Bistri%C8%9Ba.svg/200px-Letopise%C8%9Bul_de_la_Bistri%C8%9Ba.svg.png" decoding="async" width="200" height="288" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Letopise%C8%9Bul_de_la_Bistri%C8%9Ba.svg/300px-Letopise%C8%9Bul_de_la_Bistri%C8%9Ba.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Letopise%C8%9Bul_de_la_Bistri%C8%9Ba.svg/400px-Letopise%C8%9Bul_de_la_Bistri%C8%9Ba.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="511" data-file-height="736" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Cyrillic_script" title="Cyrillic script">Cyrillic</a> calligraphy in the <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Chronicle_of_Bistri%C8%9Ba&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Chronicle of Bistrița (page does not exist)">Chronicle of Bistrița</a></i>, title page</div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><a href="/wiki/File:Spiridon_Ieromonahul_-_Sf._Evanghelist_Ioan_(Tetravanghel,_1502).png" class="image"><img alt="Miniature of an old man at a writing desk" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Spiridon_Ieromonahul_-_Sf._Evanghelist_Ioan_%28Tetravanghel%2C_1502%29.png/200px-Spiridon_Ieromonahul_-_Sf._Evanghelist_Ioan_%28Tetravanghel%2C_1502%29.png" decoding="async" width="200" height="292" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Spiridon_Ieromonahul_-_Sf._Evanghelist_Ioan_%28Tetravanghel%2C_1502%29.png/300px-Spiridon_Ieromonahul_-_Sf._Evanghelist_Ioan_%28Tetravanghel%2C_1502%29.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Spiridon_Ieromonahul_-_Sf._Evanghelist_Ioan_%28Tetravanghel%2C_1502%29.png/400px-Spiridon_Ieromonahul_-_Sf._Evanghelist_Ioan_%28Tetravanghel%2C_1502%29.png 2x" data-file-width="969" data-file-height="1416" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/John_the_Evangelist" title="John the Evangelist">John the Evangelist</a>, as depicted by <i><a href="/wiki/Hieromonk" title="Hieromonk">Hieromonk</a></i> Spiridon in the gospels of Putna, 1502</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>The years following Stephen's wars against the Ottoman Empire have been described as the era of "cultural policies"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMărculeț2006189_255-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMărculeț2006189-255">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> and "great architectural upsurge".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199670–71_256-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199670–71-256">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> More than a dozen <a href="/wiki/List_of_churches_established_by_Stephen_the_Great" title="List of churches established by Stephen the Great">stone churches were erected</a> at Stephen's initiative after 1487.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199670–71_256-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199670–71-256">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> The wealthiest boyars followed him, and Stephen also supported the development of monastic communities.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671_257-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671-257">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> For instance, the <a href="/wiki/Vorone%C8%9B_Monastery" title="Voroneț Monastery">Voroneț Monastery</a> was built in 1488 and the <a href="/wiki/Tazl%C4%83u_Monastery" title="Tazlău Monastery">monastery at Tazlău</a> in 1496 to 1497.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671_257-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671-257">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The style of the new churches evidences that a "genuine school of local architects" developed during Stephen's reign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671_257-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671-257">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPop2005296_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop2005296-258">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup> They borrowed components of Byzantine and Gothic architecture and mixed it with elements of local tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671_257-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671-257">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> Painted walls and towers with a base forming a star were the most featuring elements of Stephen's churches.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPop2005296–297_259-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop2005296–297-259">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> The prince also financed the building of churches in Transylvania and Wallachia, which contributed to the spread of Moldavian architecture beyond the boundaries of the principality.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671_257-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199671-257">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> Stephen commissioned votive paintings and carved tomb stones for many of his ancestors' and other relatives' graves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles2014185_260-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles2014185-260">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> The tomb room of the Putna Monastery was built to be the royal necropolis of Stephen's family.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201499_261-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201499-261">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> Stephen's own tombstone was decorated with <a href="/wiki/Acanthus_(ornament)" title="Acanthus (ornament)">acanthus leaves</a> (a motif adopted from <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_art" title="Byzantine art">Byzantine art</a>) which became the featuring decorative element of Moldavian art during the following century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles2014106_262-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles2014106-262">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stephen also contributed to the development of historiography and <a href="/wiki/Church_Slavonic_in_Romania" title="Church Slavonic in Romania">Church Slavonic literature</a> in Moldavia. He ordered the collection of the annals of the principality and initiated the completion of at least three Slavonic chronicles,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPop2005292_263-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop2005292-263">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201478_264-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201478-264">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> noted in particular for doing away with the conventions of <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_literature" title="Byzantine literature">Byzantine literature</a>, and for introducing new storytelling canons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran196821–24_265-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran196821–24-265">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> Some portions of these historiographic texts were corrected, and perhaps even dictated, by Stephen himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDemciuc200412_190-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDemciuc200412-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> The <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Chronicle_of_Bistri%C8%9Ba&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Chronicle of Bistrița (page does not exist)">Chronicle of Bistrița</a></i>, which was allegedly the oldest chronicle, narrated the history of Moldavia from 1359 to 1506.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPop2005292_263-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop2005292-263">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201478_264-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201478-264">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMitric200413_266-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMitric200413-266">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968669_267-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968669-267">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> The two versions of the <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Chronicle_of_Putna&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Chronicle of Putna (page does not exist)">Chronicle of Putna</a></i> covered the period from 1359 to 1526, but it also wrote of the history of the Putna Monastery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPop2005292_263-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop2005292-263">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201478_264-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201478-264">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> They were accompanied by a large number of lay and religious texts (including the Gospels, in several versions by Teodor Mărișescul; as well as commentary on the <a href="/wiki/Nomocanon" title="Nomocanon">Nomocanon</a> and Slavonic translations from <a href="/wiki/John_Climacus" title="John Climacus">John Climacus</a>). Some were richly decorated with miniatures, such as portraits of Stephen (in the <a href="/wiki/Humor_Monastery" title="Humor Monastery">Humor Monastery</a> <i>Gospel</i>, 1473) and his courtier <a href="/w/index.php?title=Ioan_T%C4%83utu&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Ioan Tăutu (page does not exist)">Ioan Tăutu</a> (<i><a href="/wiki/Psalter" title="Psalter">Psalter</a></i> of <a href="/wiki/Mukachevo" title="Mukachevo">Mukachevo</a>, 1498).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMitric200413–14_268-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMitric200413–14-268">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup> The "Moldavian style", developed at <a href="/wiki/Neam%C8%9B_Monastery" title="Neamț Monastery">Neamț Monastery</a> by the disciples of <a href="/w/index.php?title=Gavriil_Uric&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Gavriil Uric (page does not exist)">Gavriil Uric</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETurdeanu1951_269-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETurdeanu1951-269">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup> became influential outside Moldavia, creating a fashion among Russian illustrators and calligraphers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMitric200414_270-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMitric200414-270">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="National_hero">National hero</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stephen_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: National hero">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

(Eminescu)">Doina</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Ipolit_Str%C3%A2mbu" title="Ipolit Strâmbu">Ipolit Strâmbu</a></div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" banknote</div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><a decoding="async" width="200" height="275" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/08-27-2016_-_%D0%9F%D0%B0%D1%80%D0%B0%D0%B4_%D0%B2_%D0%9C%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%B4%D0%BE%D0%B2%D1%8B_05.jpg/300px-08-27-2016_-_%D0%9F%D0%B0%D1%80%D0%B0%D0%B4_%D0%B2_%D0%9C%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%B4%D0%BE%D0%B2%D1%8B_05.jpg //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/08-27-2016_-_%D0%9F%D0%B0%D1%80%D0%B0%D0%B4_%D0%B2_%D0%9C%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%B4%D0%BE%D0%B2%D1%8B_05.jpg/400px-08-27-2016_-_%D0%9F%D0%B0%D1%80%D0%B0%D0%B4_%D0%B2_%D0%9C%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%B4%D0%BE%D0%B2%D1%8B_05.jpg 2x" data-file-width="698" data-file-height="960" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption">Stephen's monument in <a href="/wiki/Great_National_Assembly_Square,_Chi%C8%99in%C4%83u" title="Great National Assembly Square, Chișinău">Great National Assembly Square, Chișinău</a></div></div></div></div></div>
<p>Stephen received the sobriquet "Great" shortly after his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201475_271-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201475-271">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup> Sigismund I of Poland and Lithuania referred to him as "that great Stephen" in 1534.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199676_272-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199676-272">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup> The Polish historian <a href="/wiki/Marcin_Kromer" title="Marcin Kromer">Martin Kromer</a> mentioned him as the "great prince of the Moldavians."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201475_271-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201475-271">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199676–77_273-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199676–77-273">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Maciej_Stryjkowski" title="Maciej Stryjkowski">Maciej Stryjkowski</a>, by 1580 the Wallachians and Moldavians alike sang ballads honoring Stephen, whose portrait was displayed at the court of Bucharest; his raids in Wallachia were generally overlooked in such testimonials.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECristea2016306–307_274-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECristea2016306–307-274">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup> Despite being honored for his skill, he was still primarily known under sobriquets indicating his standing and age: in 16th-century Moldavia and Wallachia, he was casually known as <i>Ștefan cel Vechi</i> and <i>Ștefan cel Bătrân</i> ("Stephen the Ancient" or "the Old").<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMureșan2008142–143_275-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMureșan2008142–143-275">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup> Oral history also maintained Stephen's Byzantine self-references, often calling him an "emperor" or a "<i>crai</i> (king) of the Moldavians".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchipor2004200_276-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchipor2004200-276">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the mid-17th-century, Grigore Ureche described Stephen as "a benefactor and a leader" when writing of his funeral.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199676_272-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199676-272">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201477_277-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201477-277">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup> A boyar by birth, Ureche also mentioned Stephen's despotic cruelty, bad temper, and diminutive stature — possibly because, according to scholar <a href="/wiki/Lucian_Boia" title="Lucian Boia">Lucian Boia</a>, he resented authoritarian princes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia2001195–196_278-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia2001195–196-278">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup> In tandem, local folklore came to regard Stephen as a protector of peasantry against noblemen and foreign invaders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199678_279-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199678-279">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup> For centuries, free peasants claimed that they inherited their landed property from their ancestors to whom it had been granted by Stephen for their bravery in the battles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199678–79_280-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199678–79-280">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPelivanConstantinNegreiNegru201244,_85,_275,_566,_589,_591_281-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPelivanConstantinNegreiNegru201244,_85,_275,_566,_589,_591-281">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHârnea197955–57_282-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHârnea197955–57-282">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEIliescu200668–71_283-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIliescu200668–71-283">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Such precedents also made Stephen a cult figure in <a href="/wiki/Romanian_nationalism" title="Romanian nationalism">Romanian nationalism</a>, which sought the <a href="/wiki/United_Principalities_of_Moldavia_and_Wallachia" title="United Principalities of Moldavia and Wallachia">union of Moldavia with Wallachia</a>, and in rival <a href="/wiki/Moldovenism" title="Moldovenism">Moldovenism</a>. Early in the 19th century, the Moldavian regionalist <a href="/wiki/Gheorghe_Asachi" title="Gheorghe Asachi">Gheorghe Asachi</a> made Stephen the topic of historical fiction, popular prints, and heraldic reconstructions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197719–20_284-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197719–20-284">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968365–370_285-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968365–370-285">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup> Asachi, and later <a href="/wiki/Teodor_Bal%C8%99" title="Teodor Balș">Teodor Balș</a>, also campaigned for the erection of a Stephen the Great statue, which was supposed to represent resistance against Wallachian encroachment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEXenopol1910331,_339_286-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEXenopol1910331,_339-286">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup> The Moldavian separatist <a href="/w/index.php?title=Nicolae_Istrati&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Nicolae Istrati (page does not exist)">Nicolae Istrati</a> wrote several theatrical works which contributed to the Stephen cult.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968240,_581–583_287-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968240,_581–583-287">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup> Other Moldavians, shunning separatism, paid their own homage to the medieval hero. In the 1840s, <a href="/wiki/Alecu_Russo" title="Alecu Russo">Alecu Russo</a> inaugurated the effort to collect and republish folklore about Stephen, which he believed was the "source of truth" about Romanian history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchipor2004195–198_288-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchipor2004195–198-288">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup> One of the first epic poems to deal with the voivode was "The Aprod Purice", by <a href="/wiki/Constantin_Negruzzi" title="Constantin Negruzzi">Constantin Negruzzi</a>, which fictionalizes the battle of Șcheia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968382,_389_289-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968382,_389-289">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEXenopol1910211_290-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEXenopol1910211-290">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/Bessarabia_Governorate" title="Bessarabia Governorate">Bessarabia Governorate</a>, which had been carved out of Moldavia by the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a>, the peasantry and intellectual class both appealed to Stephen as a symbol of resistance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPelivanConstantinNegreiNegru201244,_85,_102,_116,_136,_158–159,_275,_362,_566,_589_291-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPelivanConstantinNegreiNegru201244,_85,_102,_116,_136,_158–159,_275,_362,_566,_589-291">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup> His "golden century" was a reference for <a href="/wiki/Alexandru_H%C3%A2jdeu" title="Alexandru Hâjdeu">Alexandru Hâjdeu</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPelivanConstantinNegreiNegru2012102_292-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPelivanConstantinNegreiNegru2012102-292">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Bogdan_Petriceicu_Hasdeu" title="Bogdan Petriceicu Hasdeu">Bogdan Petriceicu Hasdeu</a>. The latter dedicated him a large number of works, from poems written in his native Russian to Romanian-language historical novels in which Stephen is a leading protagonist.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968676–678,_686–693_293-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968676–678,_686–693-293">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By then, the cult of Stephen's "patriotic virtues" had been introduced to Wallachia by <a href="/wiki/Ien%C4%83chi%C8%9B%C4%83_V%C4%83c%C4%83rescu" title="Ienăchiță Văcărescu">Ienăchiță Văcărescu</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gheorghe_Laz%C4%83r" title="Gheorghe Lazăr">Gheorghe Lazăr</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968162,_252_294-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968162,_252-294">&#91;294&#93;</a></sup> Wallachian scholar <a href="/wiki/Nicolae_B%C4%83lcescu" title="Nicolae Bălcescu">Nicolae Bălcescu</a> was the first Romanian historian to describe Stephen as a national hero; his rule, Bălcescu argued, was an important step towards the unification of the lands inhabited by Romanians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201480_295-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201480-295">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup> During that period, Stephen became explicitly mentioned in the Romantic poetry of <a href="/wiki/Andrei_Mure%C8%99anu" title="Andrei Mureșanu">Andrei Mureșanu</a>, in particular as the "mighty shadow" described in <a href="/wiki/De%C8%99teapt%C4%83-te,_rom%C3%A2ne!" title="Deșteaptă-te, române!">Romania's future national anthem</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968570_296-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968570-296">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup> In 1850s Wallachia, <a href="/wiki/Dimitrie_Bolintineanu" title="Dimitrie Bolintineanu">Dimitrie Bolintineanu</a> produced a lukewarm ballad depicting Stephen fleeing for battle, and his mother Oltea ordering him back.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia2001207_297-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia2001207-297">&#91;297&#93;</a></sup> It became a hugely popular after being set to music.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968547_298-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968547-298">&#91;298&#93;</a></sup> His later works also contribute to the nationalist cult, or fictionalize his erotic life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968542–544,_547–548_299-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968542–544,_547–548-299">&#91;299&#93;</a></sup> The nationalist investment in Stephen was by then resisted by other writers, in particular <a href="/wiki/George_Panu" title="George Panu">George Panu</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ioan_Bogdan_(historian)" title="Ioan Bogdan (historian)">Ioan Bogdan</a>, and other <i><a href="/wiki/Junimea" title="Junimea">Junimea</a></i> members, who favored a critique of <a href="/wiki/Romantic_nationalism" title="Romantic nationalism">Romantic nationalism</a>. In Panu's works, Stephen appears as merely a "Polish vassal"; the one-time <i>Junimist</i> <a href="/wiki/Alexandru_Dimitrie_Xenopol" title="Alexandru Dimitrie Xenopol">A. D. Xenopol</a> also chided the voivode for his loss of Chilia and his supposed betrayal of Wallachia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia200157,_135,_189–190_300-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia200157,_135,_189–190-300">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Anniversaries of the most important events of Stephen's life have been officially celebrated since the 1870s,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201483_301-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201483-301">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup> including in 1871 the defiant show of solidarity at <a href="/wiki/Putna,_Suceava" title="Putna, Suceava">Putna</a>. This doubled as a protest against <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austria-Hungary</a>, which had annexed Bukovina; it was organized by <a href="/wiki/Teodor_V._%C8%98tefanelli" title="Teodor V. Ștefanelli">Teodor V. Ștefanelli</a> and was notably attended by poet <a href="/wiki/Mihai_Eminescu" title="Mihai Eminescu">Mihai Eminescu</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECândea19377–10_302-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECândea19377–10-302">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup> Nationalist interpretations still prevailed, particularly after 1881, when Eminescu dedicated his poem <i><a href="/wiki/Doina_(Eminescu)" title="Doina (Eminescu)">Doina</a></i> (written in the style of <a href="/wiki/Doina" title="Doina">traditional Romanian song</a>) to Stephen, calling upon him to leave his grave to again lead his people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201480_295-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201480-295">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia2001194–195,_213_303-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia2001194–195,_213-303">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199680_304-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapacostea199680-304">&#91;304&#93;</a></sup> His statue was ultimately raised in <a href="/wiki/Ia%C8%99i" title="Iași">Iași</a> in 1883.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201483_301-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201483-301">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968676_305-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDimaChițimiaCorneaTodoran1968676-305">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the 400th anniversary of the voivode's death in 1904, ceremonies included the completion of a stone monument in <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A2rse%C8%99ti" title="Bârsești">Bârsești</a>, by locals who claimed descent from Vrâncioaia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHârnea197957,_124,_135_306-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHârnea197957,_124,_135-306">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEIliescu200671–82_307-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIliescu200671–82-307">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup> Also then, <a href="/wiki/Nicolae_Iorga" title="Nicolae Iorga">Nicolae Iorga</a> published Stephen's biography.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201489_308-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201489-308">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup> Against Xenopol's verdict, Iorga emphasized that Stephen's victories were to be attributed to the "true unity of the whole people" during his reign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia200160_309-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia200160-309">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> Many more works of literature appeared in the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Romania" title="Kingdom of Romania">Kingdom of Romania</a> and other Romanian-inhabited regions, helping to consolidate Stephen's cultural legacy. One such contribution was the 1909 play <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Apus_de_soare&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Apus de soare (page does not exist)">Apus de soare</a></i>, by <a href="/wiki/Barbu_%C8%98tef%C4%83nescu_Delavrancea" title="Barbu Ștefănescu Delavrancea">Barbu Ștefănescu Delavrancea</a>, including advice attributed, in the public's mind, to historical Stephen:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Moldavia was not my ancestors', was not mine, and is not yours, but belongs to our descendants and our descendants' descendants to the end of time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia2001195_310-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia2001195-310">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Depicting Stephen as a dying sage, it was followed by two other Delavrancea plays, which insisted on the prince's pragmatic cruelty and the effects this had on his succession.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELovinescu1998299–302_311-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELovinescu1998299–302-311">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup> By then, Stephen as a statesman had also become a point of reference and a benchmark for the long and stabilizing rule of <a href="/wiki/Carol_I_of_Romania" title="Carol I of Romania">Carol I</a>, <a href="/wiki/King_of_Romania" title="King of Romania">King of Romania</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia2001201–203_312-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia2001201–203-312">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup> Over the following three decades, Stephen's deeds became the inspiration for literary works by Iorga, <a href="/wiki/Mihai_Codreanu" title="Mihai Codreanu">Mihai Codreanu</a>, and especially <a href="/wiki/Mihail_Sadoveanu" title="Mihail Sadoveanu">Mihail Sadoveanu</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELovinescu1998121,_178,_183,_323_313-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELovinescu1998121,_178,_183,_323-313">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup> In the 1930s, the <a href="/wiki/Iron_Guard" title="Iron Guard">Iron Guard</a> embraced Stephen the Great's cult for its own purposes, with special emphasis on his contribution as a Christian monarch.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBruja2004_314-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBruja2004-314">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The reading of Stephen as a <a href="/wiki/Romanian_nationalism" title="Romanian nationalism">pan-Romanian nationalist</a> peaked during the late stages of <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Romania" title="Socialist Republic of Romania">Communist Romania</a>. Initially, the regime looked down on Stephen's treatment of the peasantry, and only emphasized his connections with the <a href="/wiki/East_Slavs" title="East Slavs">East Slavs</a> or his clampdown against boyardom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia2001215,_219_315-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia2001215,_219-315">&#91;315&#93;</a></sup> This stance was overturned by <a href="/wiki/National_communism_in_Romania" title="National communism in Romania">national communism</a>. Initially, <a href="/wiki/Censorship_in_Communist_Romania" title="Censorship in Communist Romania">censorship</a> toned down or removed references to his legacy in <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Moldavian Soviet Socialist Republic">Soviet Bessarabia</a> or <a href="/wiki/Moldavian_Plateau" title="Moldavian Plateau">Pokuttya</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOtu201966–67_316-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOtu201966–67-316">&#91;316&#93;</a></sup> in the 1980s, however, official historians claimed that Stephen was literally a "lord of all Romanians".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia2001140,_222,_249–250_317-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia2001140,_222,_249–250-317">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup> Iorga's book has been republished several times, including on the 500th anniversary of Stephen's death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201488–89_318-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201488–89-318">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup> On the same anniversary, Stephen was presented as a symbol of "national identity, independence and inter-ethnic harmony" in the <a href="/wiki/Moldova" title="Moldova">Republic of Moldova</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201489_308-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201489-308">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup> where he also endures as the symbol of "Moldavian particularism"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia2001140_319-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia2001140-319">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> or "Moldovan patriotism".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECojocari200793_320-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECojocari200793-320">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup> Thus, Stephen was invoked by both the <a href="/wiki/Popular_Front_of_Moldova" title="Popular Front of Moldova">Popular Front of Moldova</a>, which favored Romanian identity, and the Moldovenist <a href="/wiki/Party_of_Communists_of_the_Republic_of_Moldova" title="Party of Communists of the Republic of Moldova">Party of Communists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECojocari200790–97,_100–102_321-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECojocari200790–97,_100–102-321">&#91;321&#93;</a></sup> The latter describes Stephen as "the founder of Moldavian statehood", claiming direct continuity from his principality to the present-day state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECojocari2007101–104_322-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECojocari2007101–104-322">&#91;322&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Eagles notes, "Stephen is an ever-present icon" in both Romania and Moldova: "statues of his image abound; politicians cite him as an exemplar; schools and <a href="/wiki/%C8%98tefan_cel_Mare_University_of_Suceava" title="Ștefan cel Mare University of Suceava">a university bear his name</a>; villages and the main thoroughfares of towns and cities are named after him; there is a <a href="/wiki/%C8%98tefan_cel_Mare_metro_station" title="Ștefan cel Mare metro station">Ștefan cel Mare metro station</a> in central Bucharest; and his crowned head has adorned <i>every</i> banknote in the <a href="/wiki/Post-Soviet_states" title="Post-Soviet states">post-Soviet</a> Moldovan republic".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles20141_323-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles20141-323">&#91;323&#93;</a></sup> According to a 1999 <a href="/wiki/Opinion_poll" title="Opinion poll">opinion poll</a>, more than 13% of the participants regarded Stephen the Great as the most important personality who had "influenced the destiny of the Romanians for the better".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia200117_324-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia200117-324">&#91;324&#93;</a></sup> Seven years later, during a programme called the <i><a href="/wiki/100_Greatest_Romanians" title="100 Greatest Romanians">100 Greatest Romanians</a></i> on <a href="/wiki/TVR_(TV_network)" title="TVR (TV network)">Romanian Television</a>, he was voted "the greatest Romanian of all time".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201483_301-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201483-301">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Holy_ruler">Holy ruler</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stephen_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Holy ruler">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">Saint Stephen the Great</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><a href="/wiki/File:Tablou_votiv_Manastirea_Dobrovat_1503.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Tablou votiv Manastirea Dobrovat 1503.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Tablou_votiv_Manastirea_Dobrovat_1503.jpg/220px-Tablou_votiv_Manastirea_Dobrovat_1503.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="299" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Tablou_votiv_Manastirea_Dobrovat_1503.jpg/330px-Tablou_votiv_Manastirea_Dobrovat_1503.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/0c/Tablou_votiv_Manastirea_Dobrovat_1503.jpg 2x" data-file-width="369" data-file-height="502" /></a><div class="infobox-caption">Votive depiction of Stephen at <a href="/wiki/Dobrov%C4%83%C8%9B_Monastery" title="Dobrovăț Monastery">Dobrovăț Monastery</a></div></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;">Holy Voivode</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Romanian_Orthodox_Church" title="Romanian Orthodox Church">Romanian Orthodox Church</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">Canonized</a></th><td class="infobox-data">12 July 1992, <a href="/wiki/Bucharest" title="Bucharest">Bucharest</a>, <a href="/wiki/Romania" title="Romania">Romania</a> by <a href="/wiki/Romanian_Orthodox_Church" title="Romanian Orthodox Church">Romanian Orthodox Church</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major <a href="/wiki/Shrine" title="Shrine">shrine</a></span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Putna_Monastery" title="Putna Monastery">Putna Monastery</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">2 July</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/Mount_Athos" title="Mount Athos">Athonite</a> legends, Romanian stories, and Moldavian chronicles alike, Stephen's victories against the Ottomans and Hungarians were already regarded as God-inspired, or as placed under the direct patronage of various saints (<a href="/wiki/Saint_George" title="Saint George">George</a>, <a href="/wiki/Demetrius_of_Thessaloniki" title="Demetrius of Thessaloniki">Demetrius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Procopius_of_Scythopolis" title="Procopius of Scythopolis">Procopius</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Saint_Mercurius" title="Saint Mercurius">Mercurius</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECristea2016316–340_325-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECristea2016316–340-325">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchipor2004206_326-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchipor2004206-326">&#91;326&#93;</a></sup> Worship of Stephen himself was first recorded in the 1570s,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESimon2009233_252-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESimon2009233-252">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> but, according to Ureche, he had been regarded as a saint soon after his funeral: "not on account of his soul ... for he was a man with sins ... but on account of the great deeds he accomplished".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201477_277-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201477-277">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup> The positive nuances of Ureche's report were also repeated by <a href="/wiki/Miron_Costin" title="Miron Costin">Miron Costin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201477_277-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201477-277">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The abbot of Putna Monastery, Artimon Bortnic, initiated the investigation of the tomb room of the monastery in 1851, referring to important shrines in Russia and Moldavia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles2014110_327-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles2014110-327">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup> In 1857 (a year after Stephen's tomb was opened), the priest and journalist Iraclie Porumbescu already wrote of the "holy bones of Putna".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201493_328-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201493-328">&#91;328&#93;</a></sup> In at least some legends attested by 1903, the voivode is depicted as an immortal <a href="/wiki/King_asleep_in_mountain" title="King asleep in mountain">sleeping hero</a>, or, alternatively, as the ruler of heaven.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchipor2004202_329-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchipor2004202-329">&#91;329&#93;</a></sup> However, Stephen the Great was ignored when the <a href="/wiki/Romanian_Orthodox_Church" title="Romanian Orthodox Church">Romanian Orthodox Church</a> canonized the first Romanian saints in the 1950s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoia200173_330-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoia200173-330">&#91;330&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Teoctist_Ar%C4%83pa%C8%99u" title="Teoctist Arăpașu">Teoctist</a>, <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_All_Romania" title="Patriarch of All Romania">Patriarch of All Romania</a>, canonized Stephen along with 12 other saints at <a href="/wiki/Saint_Spyridon_the_New_Church" title="Saint Spyridon the New Church">Saint Spyridon the New Church</a> in Bucharest on 21 June 1992.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamet1998195_331-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamet1998195-331">&#91;331&#93;</a></sup> On this occasion, the patriarch emphasized that Stephen had been a defender of Christianity and protector of his people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201478_264-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201478-264">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> He also underlined that Stephen had built churches during his reign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles201478_264-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles201478-264">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> Stephen's feast day is 2 July (the day of his death) in the calendar of the Romanian Orthodox Church. On his first feast after his canonization, a new ceremony was held to celebrate Stephen the Great and Saint in Putna.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamet1998195_331-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamet1998195-331">&#91;331&#93;</a></sup> 15,000 people (including the <a href="/wiki/President_of_Romania" title="President of Romania">President of Romania</a> at the time, <a href="/wiki/Ion_Iliescu" title="Ion Iliescu">Ion Iliescu</a>, and two ministers) attended the event.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamet1998196_332-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamet1998196-332">&#91;332&#93;</a></sup> Patriarch Teoctist noted that "God has brought us together under the same skies, just as Stephen rallied us under the same flag in the past."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamet1998196_332-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamet1998196-332">&#91;332&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Arms">Arms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stephen_the_Great&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Arms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Stephen's rule consolidated the usage of the <a href="/wiki/Flag_and_coat_of_arms_of_Moldavia" title="Flag and coat of arms of Moldavia">coat of arms of Moldavia</a>, featuring the <a href="/wiki/Aurochs" title="Aurochs">aurochs</a> head (first attested in 1387), sometimes as a <a href="/wiki/Helmet_(heraldry)" title="Helmet (heraldry)">helmet</a> atop his personal arms. He revived the elaborate design introduced under <a href="/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Moldavia" title="Alexander I of Moldavia">Alexander the Good</a>, which also featured a <a href="/wiki/Rose_(heraldry)" title="Rose (heraldry)">rose</a>, <a href="/wiki/Crescent" title="Crescent">crescent</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sun_(heraldry)" title="Sun (heraldry)">sun</a> and star (often, but not always, <a href="/wiki/Five-pointed_star" title="Five-pointed star">five-pointed</a>); its <a href="/wiki/Tincture_(heraldry)" title="Tincture (heraldry)">tinctures</a> remain unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197783–85,_98–120_333-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197783–85,_98–120-333">&#91;333&#93;</a></sup> This arrangement was not familiar to heraldists in Western Europe. By the 1530s, they represented Moldavia with <a href="/wiki/Attributed_arms" title="Attributed arms">attributed arms</a> featuring <a href="/wiki/Moor%27s_head_(heraldry)" title="Moor&#39;s head (heraldry)">Maures</a>; these arms, though originally used for Wallachia, possibly echoed Stephen's victories over the Ottomans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197777–81,_126_334-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197777–81,_126-334">&#91;334&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The personal arms and <a href="/wiki/Heraldic_flag" title="Heraldic flag">heraldic flags</a> used by Stephen have been the topic of additional scrutiny and debate. Stephen is known to have used a <i><a href="/wiki/Division_of_the_field" title="Division of the field">party per cross</a></i> shield with one striped quarter, but the colors are uncertain: one prevailing interpretation is that the dominant tinctures were <a href="/wiki/Or_(heraldry)" title="Or (heraldry)">or</a> and <a href="/wiki/Vert_(heraldry)" title="Vert (heraldry)">vert</a>, although they may also have been <a href="/wiki/Gules" title="Gules">gules</a> and <a href="/wiki/Argent" title="Argent">argent</a>. These may derive from the colors used by the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Basarab" title="House of Basarab">House of Basarab</a> (which were possibly used by Stephen's in-law <a href="/wiki/Radu_the_Handsome" title="Radu the Handsome">Radu the Handsome</a>), from the <a href="/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Hungary" title="Coat of arms of Hungary">coat of arms of Hungary</a>, or from a purely Moldavian tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197767–68,_107,_110_335-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197767–68,_107,_110-335">&#91;335&#93;</a></sup> The division and the striped pattern are possibly Hungarian; they survived in some of Stephen's seals even during his dispute with the Hungarian crown. He also continued to use the <i><a href="/wiki/Fleur-de-lis" title="Fleur-de-lis">fleur-de-lis</a></i>, an <a href="/wiki/Capetian_House_of_Anjou" title="Capetian House of Anjou">Angevin</a> symbol, but altered it into a "double-headed lily", then renounced it altogether.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu1977109,_111–112,_119–120_336-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu1977109,_111–112,_119–120-336">&#91;336&#93;</a></sup> Similarly, he used the <a href="/wiki/Cross_of_Lorraine" title="Cross of Lorraine">Cross of Lorraine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cross_patt%C3%A9e" title="Cross pattée">pattée</a>, possibly in reference to the <a href="/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Lithuania" title="Coat of arms of Lithuania">Pahonia</a>. Following his 1489 dispute with Poland, that charge was altered into a double <a href="/wiki/Cross_fleury" title="Cross fleury">cross fleury</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu1977118,_119_337-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu1977118,_119-337">&#91;337&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stephen's heraldic symbols progressively merged with those attributed to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Bogdan-Mu%C8%99at" title="House of Bogdan-Mușat">House of Mușat</a>, and were intensively used by all princes who claimed full or partial descent from Alexander the Good—including <a href="/wiki/Peter_the_Lame" title="Peter the Lame">Peter the Lame</a>, a Wallachian pretender to Moldavia's throne.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197796,_99–100,_102–108,_111–120_338-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197796,_99–100,_102–108,_111–120-338">&#91;338&#93;</a></sup> The Putna tombstones of Stephen's two sons who died during his lifetime, Bogdan and Peter, already display the aurochs within the "Mușat coat-of-arms".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197798–99_339-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECernovodeanu197798–99-339">&#91;339&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEagles2014102–103_340-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEagles2014102–103-340">&#91;340&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A Moldavian banner also survives in hand-colored versions illustrating <a href="/wiki/Johannes_de_Thurocz" title="Johannes de Thurocz">Johannes de Thurocz</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Chronica_Hungarorum" title="Chronica Hungarorum">Chronica Hungarorum</a></i>, with varying tinctures. These were first identified as Stephen's flags by <a href="/wiki/Constantin_Karadja" title="Constantin Karadja">Constantin Karadja</a>, and described by later authors as a version of the or-an-vert scheme in the coat of arms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETiron201271–75_341-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETiron201271–75-341">&#91;341&#93;</a></sup> Other clues suggest that the field was a solid one of or, charged with an aurochs of or, but also that the preferred "single Moldavian" color was gules.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETiron201276–80_342-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETiron201276–80-342">&#91;342&#93;</a></sup> Gules is also the color of Stephen's alleged <a href="/wiki/War_flag" title="War flag">war flag</a>, defaced with an icon of <a href="/wiki/Saint_George_and_the_Dragon" title="Saint George and the Dragon">Saint George and the Dragon</a> and donated by the prince himself to <a href="/wiki/Zograf_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Zograf monastery">Zograf monastery</a>. However, scholar Petre Ș. Năsturel cautions that this may not be a heraldic object of any kind, but rather a <a href="/wiki/Votive_offering" title="Votive offering">votive offering</a>. The "war flag", he notes, is to small to carry in battle, and does not match with images in either Thurocz or <a href="/wiki/Marcin_Bielski" title="Marcin Bielski">Marcin Bielski</a>, nor with the description in <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Guagnini" title="Alexander Guagnini">Alexander Guagnini</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENăsturel200548–49_343-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENăsturel200548–49-343">&#91;343&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

			<div class="thumb" style="width: 150px;"><div style="margin:15px auto;"><a href="/wiki/File:Moldavian_flag_in_the_Battle_of_Baia_-_bw.svg" class="image" title="Moldavian warrior and flag, uncolored version in Johannes de Thurocz. 1467"><img alt="Triangular flag with aurochs head and stripes" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Moldavian_flag_in_the_Battle_of_Baia_-_bw.svg/76px-Moldavian_flag_in_the_Battle_of_Baia_-_bw.svg.png" decoding="async" width="76" height="120" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Moldavian_flag_in_the_Battle_of_Baia_-_bw.svg/114px-Moldavian_flag_in_the_Battle_of_Baia_-_bw.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Moldavian_flag_in_the_Battle_of_Baia_-_bw.svg/152px-Moldavian_flag_in_the_Battle_of_Baia_-_bw.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="294" data-file-height="464" /></a></div></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">

		</div></li>
